<meta>The previous page introduced Saturn as the highest and slowest-moving planet, associated with the mythological figure of Cronus. This page transitions from the celestial description of the planet to its earthly correspondences, specifically listing the botanical species governed by Saturn's cold and dry influence.</meta>

<header>Saturn. ♄.</header>

# Saturn. ♄.

->The following plants are now subject to him.<-

### 1. Small Burdock / Xanthium.

<term>Small Burdock</term> <note>original: "Klein Kletten"</note> is otherwise also called <term>Beggar’s Lice</term>, <term>Knave’s Lice</term>, or <term>Hedgehog’s Lice</term> <note>These folk names refer to the way the burrs cling to clothing or fur</note>; in Greek it is called **Xanthion** <note>original: "Ζάνθιον"</note>, in Latin <term>Small Burdock</term> <note>original: "Lappa minor"</note> and <term>Inverted Burdock</term> <note>original: "lappa inversa"</note>. <term>Small Burdock</term> is similar to the <term>Round Burdock</term>; it has small, sharp <term>little burrs</term>. Its root is white and long in the earth. It bears round, elongated, prickly buttons which hang upon whatever touches them. Inside these buttons is a sweet little kernel; this makes the hair yellow. If these ripe <term>burrs</term> are opened in the Autumn and one finds two small fruit-kernels within—if they are <term>barley-kernels</term>, it signifies a fruitful year; but if they are <term>oat-kernels</term>, it signifies a dearth of all fruits. <term>Small Burdock</term> has nearly all the properties of the larger variety, especially in its seeds. The root, boiled in wine and drunk, removes the stone <note>kidney or bladder stones</note> that has lain for a long time in the loins and bladder. The root of the <term>Small Burdock</term> pounded into powder, mixed with <term>Rhubarb</term> and moistened with wine, removes all unhealthiness in the body from which <term>leprosy</term> <note>original: "Aussatz"; historically used for various chronic skin conditions</note> might otherwise arise.

### 2. Moss / Tree Moss.

There are many kinds of this genus; for some grow upon trees, some upon the ground, and some upon stones. It is nothing other than a hairy impurity and superfluity of the trees, the earth, or the stones. <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>An ancient Greek physician and botanist</note> considers the best to be that which grows on the <term>Cedar tree</term>; the next best grows on poplar trees, and among those the best is that which is white and fragrant; the black is useless. Travelers hold in high esteem that which grows in swampy places in forests, out of which grows <term>Hair-cap Moss</term> <note>original: "Widerthon"</note>, called <term>Polytrichon of Apuleius</term> <note>original: "Polytrichon Apuley"</note>. After that, there is yet another kind which is called <term>Clubmoss</term> <note>original: "Beerlach"</note>, in Latin <term>Earth Moss</term> <note>original: "Muscus terrestris"</note>, of which more shall be said hereafter. Furthermore, there is a <term>Sea Moss</term>, called <term>Sea Moss of Dioscorides</term> <note>original: "Muscus marinus Dioscoridi"</note> in his fourth book. The Latins also call <term>Musk-Moss</term> <note>original: "Bisen Muscum"</note> that which is nothing other than a secretion <note>original: "Eiter," literally "pus" or "matter"</note> of an Indian animal called the <term>Gazelle</term> or <term>Civet</term> <note>original: "Zibertus"</note>, concerning which we have been taught under the section on gums and juices. All <term>Moss</term> has an astringent nature. The white is the best, which grows on the <term>Cedar tree</term>; after that is <term>Oak Moss</term>; these resolve, open, and soften. Half a dram <note>original: "halben Quinten"</note> of the white fragrant <term>Moss</term> taken internally is good for those who have difficulty urinating. One dram is useful for those women whose <term>flowers have stopped</term> <note>A historical euphemism for suppressed or irregular menstruation</note>. Given to those with dropsy <note>original: "Wassersucht"; an old term for swelling caused by fluid retention</note> in a dose of three drams, it drives out the <term>excess fluid</term>. Thus consumed, it is also good for women who have a stillbirth remaining in the body. <term>Serapio</term> <note>Referring to the 9th-century Arab physician Ibn Sarabiyun</note> writes that wine in which white <term>Moss</term> has been steeped for several days, when drunk, induces a deep sleep. A little of said wine drunk is good for rising of the stomach <note>heartburn or indigestion</note> and for vomiting. A decoction made from <term>Moss</term>, used as a sitz bath, is good for the

<meta>catchword: Pain</meta>
<sig>A ij</sig>

<summary>This page lists plants and substances governed by the planet Saturn, specifically detailing the medicinal uses and folklore of Small Burdock (Xanthium) and various types of Moss. It emphasizes their roles in treating kidney stones, skin conditions, and reproductive health, while also noting moss's association with musk-producing animals.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Small Burdock, Xanthium, Burrs, Moss, Dioscorides, Cedar Moss, Hair-cap Moss, Musk, Civet, Serapio, medicinal herbs, astrology, dropsy, menstruation</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of earthly things governed by the planet Saturn. The previous page discussed the Small Burdock; this page concludes the botanical section with Moss and the Tamarisk, before transitioning to animals of a "Saturnine" or melancholic nature, beginning with the Bear.</meta>

...pains of the mother <note>This likely refers to uterine pains or menstrual distress</note>; **Dioscorides** <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist from the 1st century AD</note> says that surgeons and barbers also use **Moss** to stop bleeding. They have this experience from **Bears**, who, when they are wounded, still the blood with moss. The moss that grows in gardens is destroyed with slaked ashes—that is, those over which a lye has been made.

### 3. Tamarisks, <term>Tamariscus</term> <note>original: "Tamariscus"</note>.

Of its kind there are two types: the domestic and the wild. It commonly grows to the height of a man, with brown branches and leaves like other heathers, and like the **Savin-tree** <note>original: "Seven-baum"; the Savin Juniper (*Juniperus sabina*)</note>, which are ash-colored and pale green. When in bloom, it bears a mossy or hairy flower which thereafter flies away. The domestic kind brings forth its seed or fruit like **gall-nuts**, but is unknown in our regions; it grows in Syria and Egypt. The other kind, however, is found on the water-banks, such as on the Rhine. Many have held this shrub to be **Heather** <note>original: "Ericam"</note>, such as the **interpreters of Virgil** <note>scholars who commented on the works of the Roman poet Virgil</note>, where they render **lowly tamarisks** <note>original: "Humiles Myricas"</note> as heather; but this is incorrect, for heather is **Erica**. But **Myrica** is this shrub, namely the **Tamarisk**, whose figure you see illustrated here. Its nature is to cleanse and to penetrate with a constricting power. Boiled in wine and used, it opens the lungs, liver, spleen, kidneys, and bladder. Wine in which Tamarisks have been boiled is good against obstructions of the liver and spleen, and makes one urinate well. The leaves boiled in wine and drunk take away the swelling of the spleen. These roots, when drunk, remove all filthiness of the body, both internally and externally. Washing the mouth with this wine takes away the aching of the teeth and gums. **Tamarisk ash** used for lye kills lice and nits. The ash also dries up running sores. **Democritus** and **Columella** <note>Ancient Greek and Roman writers on nature and agriculture</note> write that the water in which coals of tamarisk-wood have been extinguished is healthy for pigs suffering from spleen-disease. Likewise when one makes troughs out of it and gives the pigs drink through them. It is said to do the same for humans when they drink out of a vessel made of Tamarisk-wood. **Serapio** <note>Likely referring to the medieval medical authority Ibn Sarabiyun</note> writes that he cleansed two leprous women with a boiled tamarisk-drink and raisins. For those with **jaundice** <note>original: "Gelbsüchtigen"</note>, the wine drunk from tamarisk-wood is very healthy and healing.

## Now follow those animals that have a Saturnine humor.

**Saturnine animals** are of a silent, fearful, stubborn, unrefined, lazy, and sluggish nature; they flee all company and are of impolite and distasteful manners, among which guild is found especially:

# The Bear.

Regarding which we indicated a very little a year ago, but wished to save the rest until now. The **Bear** has a weak head; for as strong a beast as he otherwise is, he has such an ingenious and weak head. If one strikes him on the head with an axe, he soon reaches his life's end; therefore, he takes no great heed if he should happen to fall from a great height, provided that he does not [fall] on his head...

Otherwise, however, he has a poisonous brain; for this reason, the ancients burned their heads so that no one might eat of them and perhaps lose their senses <note>It was a common early modern belief that certain animal organs, if prepared incorrectly or harvested from "melancholic" beasts like bears, could cause mania or poisoning.</note>. In Poland, they teach them all kinds of arts, so that they beat the drum, dance, hold a hat in their hand and beg for alms in it, wrestle with people, and let themselves be thrown down. He is also a very strong animal, for he fights with large, strong oxen, with stags, wild boars, dogs, and other beasts. He approaches them upright, and if he can only grab them by the horns with his front feet, he hangs on with his two hind feet and throws them down, tearing and killing them. If an ox is a little careless and turns its head away, the bear jumps at it and tears it down. Yet he fears the sound or noise of trumpets, kettledrums, or drums. But more on this shortly.

## A Terrifying Saturnine History <note>The term "Saturnine" refers to the planet Saturn, which in astrology and humorism was associated with dark, cruel, or tragic events, as well as the temperament of melancholy.</note> / of how Wretchedly a
### Mother Dealt with Her Two Children.

IN what **Scythian** land <note>Scythia was an ancient region north of the Black Sea, famously used by classical and early modern authors as a symbol of cold, nomadic barbarism.</note> has such a gruesome deed ever been committed, such as I will now describe? What she-wolf, **Tiger-beast**, and dragon—indeed, even the most cruel beast of the land of **Hyrcania** <note>Hyrcania was an ancient province near the Caspian Sea, legendary in literature for its ferocious tigers.</note>—can truly be compared to such a damned mother? It is an example without equal, which is scarcely to be believed, and yet it truly occurred with one named **Falante**, in a Bishop’s city in **France**.

For, when he had studied diligently in his youth, and afterwards at the university in **Toulouse** had obtained the **degree** <note>original: "Gradum"</note> in law with great praise, he went to **Rouen** <note>original: "Roan"</note>, where he gained a powerful reputation at the **Parliament** <note>In the French Old Regime, a *Parlement* was a high court of justice, not a legislative body.</note> within two or three years. Regardless of this, and although the profit therefrom was great, he nevertheless considered the great effort he had to apply: and that he otherwise had a good fortune, and thus he could well leave off **advocating** <note>working as a lawyer or advocate</note> and buy himself a seat on the Council in the said **Parliament**. He then revealed such an intention to a nobleman, who was one of his best friends, and who lived in **Calais** <note>original: "Cales"</note>, a noble city situated on the English Sea, named **Tanacre**. This man stayed more with **Falante** in **Rouen** than with his wife in **Calais**; therefore, he well begrudged his good friend **Falante** such luck, and exhorted him henceforth to proceed with his **proposal** <note>original: "Proposito"</note>. **Falante** does so, and also receives such an office of a Counselor or **Conseiller** <note>original: "Conseiller"</note> in the said **Parliament**: And he therefore sends his servant **Richard** <note>original: "Reichard"</note> to **Paris** <note>original: "Pariß"</note> to pay a **mark of gold** <note>original: "March Gold"; a specific weight of gold used for official fees</note> and to order all things belonging to his office in the **Chancery** <note>original: "Cantzley"</note>. He, however, travels with his friend **Tanacre** to his birth-city, with the intent to visit his mother and relatives before he enters upon the office: by whom, as well as by his only still-unmarried sister and all his kin, he is very well received. The local authorities themselves do him great honor

...because they had already learned that he would soon be one of the councilors of the Parliament <note>The "Parlements" in early modern France were the highest provincial courts of justice, not legislative bodies.</note>, or the highest court in the land. During the time he stayed there, however, his mother fell so deeply in love with her son's good friend, Tanacre, that she no longer had any appetite for food or drink; she imagined that she could not live without him. She then gave him everything she had. Tanacre finally noticed that this shameful old woman was inflamed with love for him. This annoyed him, and he fled the presence of this old ape <note>A common derogatory term in the 17th century for a foolish woman or one who mimics youthful behavior inappropriately.</note> as much as he could and might. But because this old woman, Gabrine, could no longer endure the flames of her love, she begged Tanacre to have pity on her. He refused the desperate woman and this shameful nest of sin. At this, she became so foolish that she wanted to kill herself. 

Nevertheless, she thought she would try once more. To this end, she spoke to Tanacre again with many words, pointing out among other things that she knew well that his fortune was not so great that he could maintain his social standing. If he would now grant her his will in this matter, he should henceforth receive so much from her that he could live in all joy and satisfaction. He should not, therefore, reject such an opportunity, which does not present itself at all times. Tanacre was finally somewhat moved and enticed by such great promises, but he still wavered, reminding Gabrine of his honor. Furthermore, he feared that he would provoke her son's wrath, especially if he were to receive some of his property, and that he would be seen as very ungrateful toward him, which he could never do. 

"All these excuses and apologies," said Gabrine, "will never extinguish a single spark of my passion. I love you so much that for your sake I would ruin not only my children, but myself. And as for my son Falante, you shall take his place and for that reason marry my daughter." 

"How could that be?" said Tanacre, "since I am married, and your son is still alive and well." 

"Rest easy," she answered, "everything shall come to an end through various means. And it is a vain thing to try to imagine another life <note>original: "ein anders Leben", likely referring to the afterlife.</note>. This is the best joy we can have in this world. For after death, nothing comes of it." 

Tanacre had been raised in war from his youth and knew little of how to speak of God and His Word, nor did he concern himself much with piety. Therefore, the old, cursed deceiver could easily persuade him. They finally concluded this devilish and damned counsel: that she would make the daughter drunk, and that in such a state of intoxication Tanacre should sleep with her; that further, the mother would poison her son, and Tanacre would poison his wife in Calais <note>original: "Cales".</note> with gift. He would then return and, eight days before the wedding, sleep with the old Gabrine, and afterward hold the wedding with the daughter and possess the estates.

Once these two had reached an agreement on a certain day, Gabrine prepared a grand banquet, persuading her son that she wanted to invite their closest friends for his sake. Falante was well pleased with this and asked his mother to spare no expense. The friends appeared, and everyone was merry and in good spirits. But when almost everyone was about to go home and they were having a final farewell-drink <note>original: "Valet-Trüncklein". From the Latin *valete*, meaning farewell.</note>, the mother gave her son a prepared drink instead of the usual wine, which was meant to take effect within two hours and finish him off <term>Garauß: a German expression meaning to "finish off" or "put an end to" something, here referring to a fatal dose of poison.</term>. To her daughter Eleonora, however, she gave only a sleeping potion, which immediately made her sleepy and dazed, as if she were drunk. Therefore, so that the guests would not notice, she led her daughter to bed early. 

When everyone was gone home, Tanacre lay down with the daughter and had his will with her, as if she were a marble statue <note>original: "Marmelsteinern Bild".</note>, for she had no consciousness because of the drink she had taken. Falante, meanwhile, had gone to rest, but it did not last long because the poison soon began to take effect. He complained; Tanacre (who, now satisfied in his beastly desires, had returned to his usual chamber to Falante) heard it and asked him how he was and if he lacked anything. 

"I fear," answered Falante, "that I have eaten something bad. Therefore, I would like someone to fetch a physician <note>original: "Medicum".</note>." 

When the mother, who had not yet gone to bed, heard this, she feared that her murderous deeds might be revealed by the physician...

When this was done, the old woman lit a fire, boiled water in a kettle, and washed Falante’s limbs in it so that the blood would no longer run; afterward, she poured the warm water onto the pavement so that no blood would be seen. Then she put all the pieces of this body into a sack and went to Tanacre, who was completely paralyzed with terror and had turned as white as snow; she kissed him and promised him that he should henceforth have all her possessions. 

When day began to break, Gabrine went into the stable, saddled his horse herself, prepared breakfast, and gave Tanacre fifty gold crowns in a pouch. She told him to be cheerful and to travel to Caen <note>original: "Cales"; likely referring to the city of Caen in Normandy, which fits the geographical context of Dieppe and Paris better than Calais.</note>, to dispose of his wife there as well, and then to return and take possession of the estates. Tanacre rode away, taking the sack with him; but as soon as he came before the city, his conscience awoke and he felt the torments of hell. He cursed the day of his birth and could no longer carry the pieces of Falante with him; instead, he threw the sack into a grain field about half a mile outside the city and rode away, finally arriving after several days in the city of Dieppe. 

But he had barely left Gabrine when Falante’s servant, named Reichard, returned from Paris, where he had successfully carried out his master’s business and intended to bring him good news. He therefore sought him in his mother's house, where he found her sitting on a small stool, still making merry over the murder she had committed and arguing with herself. He asked after his master, where he might be. Gabrine was annoyed that he had disturbed her in her murderous thoughts; therefore, she looked at him squint-eyed and said to him that he should go look for his master himself, for she was not his keeper. 

The servant did not know what to think, for this welcome pained him so much that he even said: He had expected that, because of the trouble and diligence he had applied to his master's business, he should be treated a little differently. He did not know where he stood; if he was not welcome, they might as well give him his dismissal. Meanwhile, the rumor also reaches the city; everyone runs out into the field, and the authorities themselves go there, who have the sack picked up and carried into the city. As the people were running along in great numbers, it happened that Reichard by chance came upon them, inquiring into the matter; and when he is informed of the business, the heart in his body is so moved that he must say: "Alas! from where does such a stirring come to me? Could this truly be my master’s body? God grant that it be not true." And in such thoughts, he goes with other people into the courtroom, where the sack is laid out publicly to see if anyone might be present who could recognize this person. Reichard pays close attention: and because Falante was so badly disfigured in the face by his mother that he could not be recognized, he therefore paid heed to the marks which his master had on his body and which were known to him: and when he found them, he let out a great cry, fell to the ground in a faint: and when he came to himself again, he howled and lamented so piteously that even bears and tigers should have been moved to compassion: thus there was no one among the bystanders who did not weep with him.

The judge meanwhile had Reichard lifted up again, and understood from him his journey toward Paris: likewise, how he had been received by the old Gabrine: and that he held Tanacre for the perpetrator because he had absented himself. The authorities are exceedingly stunned by this state of affairs; they have the dead man covered and proceed to Gabrine’s house. She, when she hears of her son’s death, acts as if she were very mournful. But everyone understood that these were only crocodile’s tears. They question her as to where Tanacre has traveled; she says that she does not know. Falante’s friends send out messengers here and there, and describe Tanacre, who, as was mentioned above, had meanwhile come to Dieppe, intending to sail from there to Calais <note>original: "Cales"</note>. However, since such a great storm had arisen that one could not travel upon the sea, he was forced against his will to stay there for a while. 

Once, he was strolling out to the port to see if the captain <note>original: "Patron"</note> with whom he had bargained would depart for Calais: then it happened that three sergeants <term>Scherganten</term> <note>officers of the law or bailiffs</note>, to whom Tanacre had also been described, encountered him, and after they had observed him well, they greeted him and called him by his name. And because he acknowledged it and looked around, they immediately noticed that it must be he: therefore they took him prisoner. Tanacre was terrified by this, nor did he desire to resist, but said that he well knew why they seized him: and that he had more desire to go into the prison than they had to lead him there: this only did he beg for God’s sake, that they should first lead him to the judge, for he had strange things to reveal to him. When he was then led there, he fell upon his knees <note>original: "Fußfall"; a prostration or falling at someone's feet</note> and told the said judge the whole business: who, being enraged to the highest degree and yet at the same time terrified by such an unheard-of atrocity—which should make the hair of even the most godless stand on end—immediately sent word to the city where Gabrine resided, and informed the authorities there of the business and the testimony, whereupon Gabrine was also taken into custody from that hour. 

Meanwhile, the matter reached even the Parliament at Rouen <note>original: "Roan"</note>, which had the two prisoners brought there. Tanacre there once more confessed the crime willingly and showed great signs of repentance, for which reason he also received the mercy that only his right hand was chopped off alongside his head <note>meaning he was beheaded, but lost his hand first as a standard penalty for such crimes</note>. Gabrine, however, would confess nothing, but remained in her cursed wickedness. And even though they applied the torture <term>Tortur</term> <note>judicial torture used to extract a confession</note> to her, she would only half-confess the deed: she also said that Tanacre, who was presented to her, was a fool and wanted to die willingly of his own accord. Since nothing could be achieved with her, but the deed was otherwise bright and clear, she was first dragged <note>original: "geschleifft"; a punishment where the criminal is dragged to the execution site on a sled or hide</note> and led before a church, where her right hand was chopped off. Afterwards, they led her to the place of execution, where they pricked her over her whole body with spikes and tore open her breasts with glowing pincers. Finally, she was drowned <term>gedöpfft</term> <note>likely referring to "ducking," a form of execution by drowning often reserved for women in early modern Europe</note>, the body burned, and the ashes scattered into the air. During the torture, she looked like a Fury or a tormenting devil, and gave no sign of genuine repentance. Thus, these two persons have been punished in this world according to their merit: and Gabrine will first realize now whether another life is still to be hoped for after this one, since she would believe in none.

# The Second Chapter on Jove ♃.

Jupiter, counting from above, is Saturn’s neighbor in the order of the spheres, and thus the second; however, he shines much more brightly, larger, and more beautifully, as many saw in the 47th year <note>referring to 1547</note> in January, in the evening after sunset, how magnificently he shone together with Mars. The Almighty and wisest Creator placed him so near below Saturn, moving downwards toward us, so that with his noble and precious nature—that is, the <term>Sanguine</term> <note>The humor associated with blood, traditionally considered warm, moist, and cheerful</note> nature, which consists of tempered warmth and moisture—he might somewhat moderate or even prevent Saturn’s terrible malice, and especially his coldness and poison. He appears in the form of a royal, princely high official, a jurist or man learned in the law, a councilor, a mayor, or a similar honorable and stately person, clothed in a beautiful purple-colored robe, likewise holding a judicial staff in his hand, while another nobleman reaches him a golden bowl full of precious stones and gifts. He travels upon an honorable, magnificent chariot, which is drawn by two flying horses. He is also otherwise called <note>original: "Iuvans Pater"</note> "Helping Father," because by his good nature he is very beneficial to mankind and either lessens or even drives away illnesses. In the human body, he rules especially over the liver, as the workshop of the blood, as well as the good blood itself and the pulse. By the magnificent chariot upon which he travels, "good government" is signified (provided it is managed by truly Jovial persons—that is, those who love the fear of God, justice, honor, discipline, and virtue). The two peacocks on the chariot with their shining mirrors signify a well-ordered government, which shall gleam and shine with all kinds of virtues. He is otherwise called by the astrologers <note>original: "Fortuna major"</note> "the Great Fortune," because next to God, the <note>original: "Summo bono"</note> "Supreme Good," wherever he is well-placed in the <term>nativities</term> <note>birth charts</note> or otherwise, he signifies luck, health, peace, and wealth. The colors of his plants are generally purple or violet-brown, brownish-red, resin-red, and also light yellow or flesh-colored. The plants commonly have even, smooth leaves, a lovely and healthy scent, and a cheerful form and appearance. They grow in good, gentle, well-cultivated places, especially where they are exposed to the sun; they generally have fatty, oily sap and are healthy wound-herbs, both for the outside and the inside of the body. His metal is tin. Now I will proceed and once again (as before) recount three of the Jovial plants, which are:

## Catalog of Jovial Herbs. 1. Barberry, *Versich*.

*Versich* is called Barberry and *Erbsal*. It grows in bushes and rough places, with many small branches which are set with little thorns, usually two, three, or four together. It is not unlike the wild pear tree, though it is smaller and thornier. The fruit is like the myrtle, red and soft, and has its small seeds inside. The leaves resemble quince leaves but are narrower, greener, and more delicate; the root is deep and very split. Barberry is cold and dry in the second degree and is astringent. The fruit also has a piercing nature. If the juice is drunk...

The bark of the roots, likewise crushed and applied to where an arrow has been shot in, draws it out without pain. Likewise a thorn, nail, or glass, if the fruit is crushed and placed upon it. Whoever rubs their belly with <term>Barberry juice</term> <note>original: "Versich"; likely referring to verjuice or a tart preserve made from the berries</note> will be made to sweat. When <term>Barberry juice</term> is consumed, it takes away thirst and strengthens the stomach and liver. It is also good for those who have hot sores within them. Likewise, inside the body, it takes away the heat and all sickness that comes from great heat of the blood; it brings great strength to the heart and an appetite for eating. From the <term>Barberry</term> fruit, make a syrup with sugar, or a wine to be kept over the year; it is good against fever and heat of the liver, quenches thirst, stills diarrhea <note>original: "Bauchfluß"</note> and the menstrual flow <note>original: "Frawenzeit"</note>, and strengthens the stomach and liver. It is also preserved in honey or sugar. The little berries are also kept dried; one soaks them again in fresh water as needed. For constant vomiting in hot ailments, or the gagging of bile, one also uses <term>Barberry</term> <note>original: "Saurach"</note>. The young leaves in May provide a good sauce for roasted meats.

## 2. St. John’s Currants / Ribes.

**St.** <term>John’s Currants</term> <note>original: "Johannes Träublein"; referring to Red Currants, *Ribes rubrum*</note> are well known to everyone at this time; they are so named because they become ripe in the summer around that same time <note>St. John’s Day, June 24th</note>. Among the Latin names, I know no other name than that it is called <term>Ribes</term> by everyone. They grow up like <term>Gooseberries</term> <note>original: "Grosselbeeren"</note>, but without thorns, and are planted at the edges of gardens for pleasure. Its little branches are tough and brown; the leaves resemble grapevine leaves but are smaller and dark green. It brings forth its pale yellow, star-shaped little flower in May, from which green, round little berries first develop, and afterwards a beautiful bright red, hanging on thin, long stems. They have a lovely wine-sour taste. The wild kind is not very different from the cultivated, but has an unpleasant taste and grows by water streams. Furthermore, one finds in Alsace, and also in other places, a shrub that brings red berries like coral, usually two together like twins; they are equal in size to those mentioned before, and give a sweetish taste at first, but cause nausea and vomiting. The bark of the trunk is ash-colored; the leaves are entirely grey on one side, like quince leaves, but are longer, almost like olives. The fruits and leaves cool and dry in the second degree and are astringent. These little currants have all the power of <term>Barberry</term> or <term>Barberry juice</term>, except that they are milder and more pleasant. The currants boiled with <term>Sorrel</term> <note>original: "Sauerampffer"</note> are good against thirst and the <term>Plague</term> <note>original: "Pestilentz"</note>. This drink is also good against drunkenness and the flow of the <term>golden veins</term> <note>original: "güldin Adern"; a traditional term for hemorrhoids</note>. From these currants, one makes an <term>electuary</term> <note>original: "Latwergen"; a medicinal paste made with honey or syrup</note> and syrup; it is very good for "hot" people <note>those with a choleric or sanguine temperament</note>. A drink made from <term>St. John’s Currants</term> and <term>endive water</term> is good against redness and the purples <note>original: "Purpeln"; likely referring to purpura or skin rashes</note>.

# 3. Peach-wort / Persicaria.

**P**each-wort has many names; it is also called Fleawort, Fly-herb, Water-pepper, Pain-nettle, and Peacock-herb. There are two kinds: the male or the large, and the female or the small. The large Peach-wort, or the male, which is specifically called Peach-wort, is an herb that grows up from seed every year like the <term>clear-nettle</term> <note>original: "Heyternessel"</note>; it grows two spans high, sometimes lower, and flourishes where it has a rich and fertile place. The leaves are like the foliage of willow and peach trees; some are speckled as if a drop of blood had fallen on each, which one may also notice on some dead-nettles, the <term>Cuckoo-pint</term> <note>original: "Aron"</note>, the <term>Satyrione</term> <note>an orchid species</note>, and Buttercup. The stem of this herb is knotted like other crops, such as rye or oats. The little flowers are crowded together, like a pointed little bunch of grapes or an ear of grain; one kind has flesh-colored flowers, the second has <term>crimson-red</term> <note>original: "Presilgfarben"; the color of brazilwood dye</note> little flowers, and the third has completely snow-white little flowers. All three produce a beautiful, broad, brown seed, and all are hot on the tongue. All three grow in damp places, such as by ponds, puddles, pools, etc. This is a precious Fly-herb, for whatever wound on an animal is anointed with the juice of these herbs, no fly or gnat will ever sit upon it again, no matter how hot the summer may be. The second Peach-wort is exactly like the previous one, except that it is smaller in all its form and tastes almost like sorrel; <term>Female Water-pepper</term> <note>original Latin: "Hydropiper fœmina"</note>.

->Now follows the Jovial animal.<-

**S**ince we considered the roe deer a year ago with three parables of spiritual wisdom, and applied such to a Christian, godly wife—a comparison that particularly pleased me—I could not pass by without bringing another parable to the path and thus making the conclusion. Even as the naturalists write of the roe deer and hinds, and as the old church teacher <term>Augustine</term> <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo</note> mentions among others in <term>Volume 4, Book 83 of Different Questions, Question 71, page 419</term> <note>original Latin: "Tom.4. lib. 83. Quæstionū, quæst. 71. fol. 419."</note>: when they wish to swim across the sea for the sake of their pasture, they swim in a row, almost as cranes fly, one after another in order; yet always the one behind lays its muzzle upon the back or <term>rump</term> <note>original: "Zemmel"</note> of the one in front. But when the one in front becomes tired, it turns back so that it too can lay its head upon another, and another must swim in front, until they finally reach land.

<meta>The text transitions from a natural history allegory about the roe deer—an animal often depicted in medieval lore as helping its companions cross rivers by resting their heads on one another—to the moral duties of marriage. From this image—as the aforementioned holy teacher of the Church <note>Likely referring to St. Jerome or St. Augustine, who often used animal allegories</note> believes—St. Paul took the saying in Galatians 6: *Bear one another’s burdens.* This should happen especially in the state of marriage, where a Christian wife should be patient and gentle, bearing her husband's ways and weaknesses with patience. She should also not let the entire weight and burden of the household and domestic cares lie upon her dear husband alone for him to carry, but should take a good portion upon herself. She should help him carry, care, and work, showing herself in this to be a faithful helper and a pillar in whom the man can find comfort and upon whom his heart may rely.

## Example of Christian Virtue of a Jovial Prince, named
### Philip the Magnanimous, late Landgrave of Hesse.

**T**he Christian rule and military deeds of this highly-praised Prince—which began even in his blossoming youth—have been described by highly learned and distinguished men, among others, as follows: That at the very beginning of his princely reign, in what were almost his childhood years—namely, when he was only thirteen years of age—he was an upright, wise, and joyful lord. He maintained good peace and safe passage in his land (though it was full of forests and stones) such as hardly any other Prince of the Empire could achieve even at great cost. Because of this, traveling merchants and traders could conduct their business and move about without being robbed by rogues. For if anyone were dispossessed and plundered of his property, the Prince would immediately have such <term>highwaymen</term> <note>original: "Schnaphänlein"; literally "snap-cocks," a term for mounted bandits or marauders who preyed on travelers</note> seized, cast into prison, and punished. He did just as his father had done, who once returned 3,000 Guilders to a man who had been robbed on Hessian soil; once he had identified the perpetrator, the father devastated and destroyed that man’s castle. Even if the culprit did not yield, corporal punishment nonetheless followed and was carried out. They sat in their positions, he said, to provide everyone with protection and defense and to keep the roads clear. He had a "Hessian head" <note>An idiom implying he was determined, stubborn, or possessed a strong will</note> and could not remain idle; he had to have something to do. However, he did not easily start things; but what he set out to do, he was not easily turned or swayed from, as his old counselor, <term>Hans von Boineburg</term> <note>original: "Beimelberg"; a prominent knight and advisor to the Landgrave</note>, also said. Furthermore, regarding what he decided should be done, he consulted with the elders and those who had understanding about the beginning and the progress—that is, how and by what means the work was to be executed—letting them advise and speak to him. For his age, he was an excellently courageous prince, a warrior and an <term>Arminius</term> <note>A reference to the Germanic hero who defeated the Roman legions; a common comparison for a great German military leader</note>. He was not of a particularly large or strong body, but in counsel and understanding, he was mighty and blessed. In his great military campaigns, he therefore always gathered a primary good hope in God's assistance and help, so that he had to arm himself for defense, saying:

> It does not depend on having many people and great, costly war-gear, but on a good cause.

Among his campaigns, the first two were led at different times against <term>Franz von Sickingen</term> <note>A famous Imperial Knight who led the Knights' Revolt</note> in the years 1518 and 1523.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

To quiet the Peasants’ Rebellion <note>The German Peasants' War of 1524–1525, a widespread popular revolt in the German-speaking areas of Central Europe</note>, he undauntedly appeared in the field in his own right in the year 1525. Afterwards, he marched against the Bishops in the year 1528. He restored the Duke of Württemberg <note>Ulrich, Duke of Württemberg, who had been exiled</note> by force in the year 1534. He defended the land of Braunschweig <note>Brunswick</note> in the year 1552. In the year 1545, he brought the Duke Henry of Brunswick <note>original: "H. H. B." (Herzog Heinrich von Braunschweig)</note> into his own hands <note>captured him</note> in the open field. Finally, he was among the ranks of the Protestants <note>The Schmalkaldic League, the defensive alliance of Lutheran princes</note>. Throughout all this, as just mentioned, he trusted and took comfort in his good cause. This was also true against the "mad rabble" <note>original: "tollen Cunken"—a derogatory term for the rebellious peasant mobs</note>, that is, the insurgent peasants already mentioned, who had already raged as far as Rotenburg on the Fulda and had taken Hersfeld; when they retreated from there, he won back the Abbey and the city of Fulda. 

But before they attacked the arrogant peasants at Frankenhausen <note>The Battle of Frankenhausen (May 1525), one of the most significant battles of the Peasants' War</note>—though his father-in-law the Duke of Saxony and Duke Henry of Brunswick were present in person to lend him assistance—and though he was at that time only twenty-one years old, he nevertheless delivered a fine oration <note>a formal speech</note> to his soldiers in the presence of the two princes concerning the cause of his undertaking. I omit this here because Sleidanus <note>Johannes Sleidanus (1506–1556), the famous historian of the Reformation</note> records it word for word in his book under the year 1525; whoever wishes may read it there. 

When he was already on the march to restore the Duke of Württemberg, people of no small authority and standing opposed him most strongly. They urged him to consider that he should not, with this war, knock over the teachings of the Gospel or bring a stain upon our doctrine—indeed, that he should not lead the entire land of Hesse into ruin. Because he had a sincere heart, he spoke: "Let it proceed now; I will not bring you to ruin." He then carried it out successfully and with power. Whoever desires to know the principal cause for undertaking such a war and restoration should read the *Polemica* <note>polemical or controversial books</note> and disputation books issued between Hesse and Braunschweig in the years 1538 and 1539; from those, as well as from what followed in the year 1542 regarding the taking of the lands of Brunswick and Wolfenbüttel, he may draw the true foundation.

### The Third Chapter: Of Mars, ♂.

In the order from the top down, Mars is the third planet, the second from Saturn, and the one nearest to Jupiter. He has been depicted by the ancient sages in the form of a terribly angry, lean, red-colored, armed man or soldier—a general or military commander. He has small but shimmering eyes, a short reddish beard, but long, red, somewhat curly hair. He is armored with a cuirass <note>a piece of armor consisting of a breastplate and backplate fastened together</note> as just mentioned; in his right hand he carries a battle-sword—though in this day and age, perhaps better a loaded musket <note>original: "geladen Rohr"—literally a 'loaded pipe', referring to early firearms</note>—and in his left hand a blood-banner with leaping flames. Otherwise, he is strong enough in his arms, legs, and other limbs. The war-chariot on which he rides is entirely plated with iron and is drawn by two strong, cunning foxes—as they are the most deceitful of animals. He is a robber, a murderer, an unmerciful burner, and a destroyer of land and people. He has his chief seat in the human body primarily in the <term>gall</term> <note>the bile; in the theory of the four humors, the gall bladder was the source of yellow bile, which caused anger</note>; for this reason, one is accustomed to say of a poisonous, very angry person that "his gall is overflowing." For his <term>humor</term> <note>In ancient and medieval medicine, one of the four bodily fluids that determined temperament</note> is...

the Cholera <note>Referring to Yellow Bile, one of the four humors, associated with a hot and dry temperament</note> / from which the word "rumbling" <note>original: "kollern," referring to the gurgling of the bowels or a raging disposition</note> originates / its metal is primarily iron / steel / from which muskets / pistols / spears / swords / daggers / armor and all murderous weapons are made / it rules over everything that is fiery and hot in the extreme degree / especially in diseases / its color is fire-colored / blood-red or what tends toward it / as are also its plants / which either bear very small flowers (like those of Saturn) or else poor ones / where if not the blossom / yet the leaves are marked with some red color and at the very least with reddish streaks / also their sap is for the most part corrosive / such as eating / piercing / burning / biting / sharp / with the most subtle heat / which drives out sweat. ## Register of Martial Herbs.

### 1. Euphorbium, Euphorbium.

**E**uphorbium or Euforbium, has its name among the Greeks / Latins / and Arabs <note>original: "Arabibus"</note> from Euphorbus, the physician <note>original: "Medico"</note> of King Juba of Mauritania / who first discovered this herb. It is a foreign plant / which comes from the New Indies <note>The Americas</note> / whose sap / also called Euphorbium / is used in many compositions in the apothecaries. It has long / broad / thick leaves / which are set on both sides with long white thorns / and many crooked / thick / divided roots. If one breaks off the leaves of this plant / and sticks one of them into the earth / it grows / and multiplies / just like the *Opuntia* / or Indian Figs <note>Prickly Pear cactus</note> / so that perhaps twenty or thirty leaves come from it.

From these leaves / if one scores them / is collected the sharpest / burning / fiery sap / called by the same name Euphorbium. See further on this Rembert Dodoens <note>original: "Rembertum Dodonæum," a famous Flemish physician and botanist</note>, in book 2, chapter 20, *On Purgative Herbs* <note>original: "de purgantibus herbis"</note>, and Matthias de l'Obel <note>original: "Mathiam Lobel"</note> in *Observations of Plants* <note>original: "Stirpium observationibus"</note>, both of whom write / that they have the illustration of this plant from the famous Mr. Jean Boisot <note>original: "Io Boisot"</note>, who obtained it from the New Indies / and had it growing in his garden in Brussels in the year 1570. It is described by Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> / that it grows like the *Ferula* <note>Giant Fennel</note> / which, however, does not compare to this at all / from which it is to be inferred / that the herb was unknown to Dioscorides / although the sap was brought out and used at that same time / just as it is now.

### 2. Raphanus, Radish.

**T**o hardworking people the radish is harmful / yet it drives the urine / and softens the belly. Those with dropsy <note>Edema or swelling caused by fluid retention</note> may like it / and also women / whose

...understood as blood / used. Radish crushed with honey / applied over blemishes and spots in the face / drives them away / also makes fallen hair grow again. Radish juice boiled with honey / mixed with wine vinegar / and strained through is good against quartan fever <note>A fever that recurs every fourth day, historically associated with malaria</note> / and drunk against obstruction of the spleen. Radish boiled in water / with honey added / drunk for several days / drives away an old cough / drives away the stubborn phlegm in the chest / and causes one to expectorate <note>original: "außwerffen," to cough up mucus</note>. The rind of radish crushed / taken with vinegar and honey / causes vomiting. A good portion of radish cut into thin slices / with white sugar sprinkled over them / and well-pressed / then left to stand for 24 hours / and the water that one finds therein / put into a small glass / and drunk warm from it morning and evening / is a special experiment for an old cough and lung consumption <note>original: "Lungensucht," often referring to tuberculosis</note>. Its root, chopped small and distilled <note>original: "gebrandt," meaning to distill into a medicinal water</note> at the beginning of September <note>original: "Herbstmonat"</note>. This water, drunk for thirty days / every morning and evening, three or four loth <note>A historical weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce per loth</note> each time / is good for the stone <note>Referring to kidney or bladder stones</note> / promotes urination / moves the kidneys and bladder / and the places where the stone is located. This water, drunk at a dose of three loth / is good for poison / if a person has eaten or drunk it. This water is good for jaundice <note>original: "Geelsucht"</note> / kills the roundworms in the belly / makes the stomach digest well / cleanses it of all superfluous sliminess / dampness / and whatever may hinder a person's digestion. It opens all obstructions of the internal organs and veins. This water, drunk as described above / thins the slimy dampness in the lungs.

### 3. URTICA, Nettles.

The family of nettles is large / and well known to everyone. First, there are the great common stinging nettles / with high, long stems / and feathery seeds / Great Nettle <note>original: "Urtica major"</note>. After that are the small "pus-nettles" <note>Referring to the Small Stinging Nettle</note> / Small Nettle <note>original: "Vrtica minor"</note>. Thirdly / the Roman Nettle / which brings its seeds in husks / just like linseed / Roman Nettle <note>original: "Urtica Romana"</note>. These first three types / all burn and sting / and are comprised under the name Acalyphe <note>A Greek term for nettle</note> or Nettle <note>original: "Vrtica"</note> / the following types do not burn or sting. Fourth is the *Bynsang*. It is called *Binsaug* <note>Dead-nettle, likely Lamium</note> / because the bees seek and suck from the little flowers / which grow in rings around the stem / and are partly white / and partly brown and red / and yellow.

Nettles and their seeds are hot at the beginning of the first degree / and dry in the second degree. They are of many kinds. The seed is more useful than the herb / it should be collected from the "foreign" <note>original: "Welschen," often meaning Italian or southern</note> nettles / which are grown in gardens. The leaves and seeds of this herb are used in medicine / which by nature penetrates the dampness of the human body / and especially through sweating. Nettle seed is good for the stone / especially in the loins. Nettle seed powdered and drunk with wine / drives out the stone in the loins. Nettles boiled with wine / and drunk thereafter / drives away the cough. The skin washed with it / heals the evil mange <note>original: "Grind," referring to scabs or skin eruptions</note>. The seeds crushed / mixed with honey / and thus used with wine / takes away the old cough / and clears the chest.

## List of Martial Animals.

A wild boar is called *Aper* in Latin, as if it were *Afer*, from *feritas* <note>original: "à feritate," meaning from ferocity</note>, from which the German names *Eber* [boar] and *Behr* [bearish/fierce] come, because it is an entirely wild, untamed, and wicked animal. Or it comes more likely from the Greek <term>kapros</term> <note>original: "καπρος"</note> if one only removes the "k." Otherwise, it is called *porcum agrestem* or *sylvestrem*, a field pig or forest pig. A *porcus Sylvestris* is a shoat <note>original: "Frischling," a young wild boar under one year old</note> or young piglet. The Romans called a full-grown, old one a "tusked boar." Martial says: "The wild boar is defended by its teeth; the horns defend the deer" <note>original: "Dentetimetur aper, sagt Martialis, defendunt cornua cervum"</note>. And Ovid: "Fierce boars have lightning in their hooked teeth" <note>original: "Fulmen habent acres in aduncis dentibus apti"</note>. The wild boars strike about with their long teeth like bolts of lightning, as we might say. See Pliny: Book 2, Series part 2, chapters 51, 229, 230, 231, 232, 233, and chapter 52. Or *Aper*, as if *asper* [rough], because it lives or runs in *locis asperis* <note>original: "in sharp or rough places"</note>, or because it is a coarse, desolate, and wild animal. I say this for the sake of the young people, who must learn their grammar alongside their household management.

The wild boar is indeed a very wicked creature. All other animals become mild, humble, and quiet when they are castrated, but a wild boar does not do so easily. As soon as it gets its large, striking tusks, it becomes bold, wild, and foolhardy; it leaves the herd and eats alone. When a hunter approaches it with a pointed boar-spear <note>original: "Schweinspieß," a heavy spear with a crossbar to prevent the boar from sliding up the shaft toward the hunter</note>, it not only fears him not at all, but it runs straight and unafraid into the spear. And even if it is run through and not hit quite right, it always presses further onto the spear and would gladly pay its respects with its sharp teeth. It has two large, curved, strong, sharp teeth in its mouth or snout, with which it strikes in two and tears everything it encounters; for these are its sword and weapon upon which it relies, just as a buck relies on its horns. 

The "Hawer" or "Eber" [male boars] are particularly wicked and strike powerfully with their teeth, but always in an upward motion. Therefore, they cannot do anything to someone who is lying down, but only to someone standing up. Thus, whoever cannot escape must "hold a pluck" <note>an old idiom likely meaning to stand one's ground or face the music</note>, should lay himself down and take a "friendly step" as a favor <note>an ironic way of saying to accept being stepped on</note>, letting himself be trodden upon a little. However, he should look closely to see that it is indeed a male boar. For he would be badly deceived if it were a sow. For a sow can bite and tear downward with her teeth, but she can do little harm to someone standing upright. They are particularly ill-tempered during mating <note>original: "in coitu"</note> and when one does not leave their sow and young piglets in peace; for these, the boar fights powerfully and is also stronger and bolder. A wild sow is not as bold and strong as a male boar, though she is very wicked when she has farrowed [given birth] or when one has angered her and shown or plagued her young piglets.

There is a great enmity between the wolf and the wild boar, for the wolf takes their young and eats them up. The boars cannot tolerate this, so they "patch his pelt" <note>an idiom for attacking or beating someone</note> in return whenever they catch him. When they notice that the...

...wolves or hunters are present, they go before their piglets and fight for them as long as they are able. When they are otherwise required to fight, they whet their teeth on trees—just as one whets a knife on a stone—and then test them against the tree to see if they are sharp enough. If they notice that they are still blunt, they seek out **origanum**, **wild marjoram**, or **oregano** <note>original: "Origanum, Wolgemuth oder Dosten"; these are various names for the same aromatic herb, *Origanum vulgare*, believed in folklore to strengthen animal teeth</note>; from these, their teeth become clean and strong. They also rub themselves against trees so that they become covered in resin, cool themselves in the mud, and dry themselves off again in the sun; this is their armor, with which they encounter their enemies. On their right side, they have a hard and thick bone which they always hold in front of the **boar spear** <term>Schweinspieß: a heavy spear with a crossbar used for hunting dangerous game</term> like a shield; if one strikes it with the spear, it knocks the hunter over and over and lays him on his back.

## Description of a Martial History.

**A**fter this praiseworthy King of France, **Henry II** <note>original: "Heinrich der ander"</note>, at the request of the Spanish King's ambassadors, betrothed his legitimate daughter <note>Elisabeth of Valois</note> to the aforementioned Spanish King <note>Philip II</note>, and in his stead to **Don Ferdinand, Duke of Alba** <note>original: "Don Fierand Duc Dalve"</note>, etc., in the presence of the **Cardinal of Lorraine** and nearly all the princes and lords in France, etc., on June 22nd. Then, great banquets, joy, and hearty dances, along with other merry things, were prepared. From June 23rd to the 28th, all kinds of pastimes were undertaken. On June 28th, at six o'clock in the evening, the **Dauphin** <note>the heir to the French throne</note> and the **Duke of Lorraine**, together with some of the most noble lords of the Crown of France—roughly 43 in number—well-adorned with strange and rare inventions <note>referring to elaborate pageant costumes or floats</note>, along with those who waited upon them on horse and foot, very merry to behold, moved onto the track. There, each had to carry his coat of arms before him and bring it along, hanging them up publicly to announce themselves to the judges for the jousting on the following day. The King, the **Duke of Guise**, the **Prince of Ferrara**, and the **Duke of Namur** were the challengers <note>original: "Beruffer," the hosts who take on all comers</note> of the joust, as is customary for those of the **knightly estate** <note>original: "Ritterstand"</note> to endure. 

Thus, on the 29th, the Dauphin himself, well-armed and magnificently adorned, moved forth with 22 men; before him, however, went a beautiful ship, complete with three masts, covered inside and out entirely with red velvet, the sails made of silver tinsel, and all the rope and tackle belonging to the ship prepared from pure gold. The sailors were dressed in brown velvet, and many other magnificent spectacles were seen across the track, sailing as if with a headwind. Soon thereafter, the four challengers—among whom was the King himself—each performed five bouts <note>original: "5. Treffen," or passes with the lance</note> against every man who moved against them, there before everyone and before the royal ladies in great number. That same day ended happily and well. After supper, however, a hearty dance was held once more; but before the dance, the King's sister <note>Margaret of Valois</note> was married to the **Duke of Savoy** in a similar, though somewhat less grand, fashion. On the 30th, the King, together with the three aforementioned princes, making four in total, once again came onto the track as challengers of the joust in royal finery, awaiting any of the knightly estate to endure and wait as they had the previous day. This was undertaken in a knightly manner, and many brave bouts...

<summary>This page describes the defensive biological traits of the wild boar and begins a historical account of the 1559 tournament in France held to celebrate the Peace of Cateau-Cambrésis and royal marriages.</summary>

<keywords>wild boar, animal defenses, Henry II of France, Duke of Alba, jousting, tournament of 1559, Dauphin, chivalry, 16th-century heraldry</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>continuity: This page continues the account of the festivities in Paris following the betrothal of the French princess Elisabeth to Philip II of Spain. The previous page ended with the arrival of the Spanish proxy, likely the Duke of Alba, to celebrate the alliance.</meta>

...the events proceeded, and one party after another rode out to tilt. It so happened that the **Constable** <note>Anne de Montmorency (1493–1567), a high-ranking French diplomat and soldier</note> took notice that it was an unusually warm day and that the King was overexerting himself beyond his strength. In humble submission, he pleaded with the King to cease for his own sake and retire to rest, reminding him of the great labor he had endured the previous day. Furthermore, he even caught hold of the King’s **tilting spear** <term>Spieß: here referring to a heavy jousting lance</term>, refusing to allow him any further runs. However, the King replied that he had already completed four **courses** <note>original: "Curzier"; these are individual passes or bouts in a jousting tournament</note> and wished to complete a fifth for the sake and pleasure of the **Duke of Savoy** <note>Emmanuel Philibert, whose marriage to Henry's sister Margaret was also being celebrated</note>. He immediately dispatched the Constable's son to the Duke of Savoy to announce that, although he had exerted himself with no small effort both yesterday and today in these knightly games and had already finished four courses, he intended to undertake a fifth specifically in the Duke's honor.

This was carried out accordingly. One of the King's own household, a **Captain of the Guard** whom he had compelled and forced into the task—namely, the **Count of Montgomery**—engaged him in such a violent clash that the Captain’s spear shattered against the King’s breast. As it broke, a long **splinter** <note>original: "Schifer"; a wooden shard or fragment</note> broke away from the bottom of the piece that had snapped off. This shard traveled up the shaft and passed through the King's visor, entering his forehead between both eyes and grazing one eye. When the King felt the heavy blow, he staggered; he was caught and held by the thigh by one of his **pages** <note>original: "Kammerjungen"; young noble attendants</note> until the Constable and other great lords could arrive. They brought the King down from his horse to sit upon a chair and opened his small helmet, only to find it filled with blood and to discover the severity of the wound.

As they carried him toward the palace on the chair, such distress and misery arose that the Queen <note>Catherine de' Medici</note>, out of immense grief and fainting from the shock, tore her jewels, royal ornaments, and even her clothes from her body. Likewise, the other royal ladies raised a great cry and lamentation. Similarly, the **Dauphin** <note>the future Francis II</note> was led from the field by two men, overcome by sorrow and sickness. All the other persons who had gathered for this knightly tournament withdrew in great affliction; even the trumpeters departed in silence. There was such a scene of misery, lamentation, and weeping that it cannot be adequately described.

When the King had been brought to his chamber to have his wounds dressed, and the Queen had joined him, he asked for a drink and said: He had always striven to die an honorable, knightly, and manly death in war for his land and people. Although that had not been granted to him, if our Lord God wished to call him away in this manner, he would be committed to Him and His divine will be done. Despite the fact that the physicians sought to heal the wounds in haste with their special arts, this mighty King passed away on the 11th of July [1559] due to a sudden, violent fever. He was mourned by all as a gracious, friendly, and sociable King and Lord. The Count of Montgomery, who had performed the clash with the King so unwillingly and under compulsion, died shortly thereafter from grief <note>Montgomery actually survived for years but was eventually executed in 1574; the author here reflects the contemporary rumor that he perished of sorrow</note>.

## The Fourth Chapter on the Sun. ☉

**SOL**, <note>Latin: "Sol" (Sun). The author references a common historical etymology: *sol quasi solus*, meaning "the sun, as if alone," implying its unique and singular majesty.</note> is the king of all planets (next to God), a cause of light, day, and life; a noble steward or administrator of God the Most High, from whom all other creatures must take, draw, and fetch their light, warmth, power, and beauty. Its seat in the human body is primarily in the heart—which, according to the opinion of some philosophers, is the first part to live and the last to die—although in the study of **nativities** <term>Nativitäten: Birth charts or horoscopes used in astrology to determine a person's character and destiny.</term> it is held that it also commands the right eye of man. It is opposed to all injustice, as well as to devilish phantoms, thefts, sorceries, adultery, murder, etc., as these are "works of darkness" (Romans 13). Likewise, it stands against their accomplices: hags, witches, and ghosts. For in such clear, bright-shining light, these beings—who are by nature poor, blinded, and ignorant—cannot so easily deceive people. Indeed, the work itself bears grand witness to this: as soon as the mighty Sun, through the grace of the Most High, lets its rays be seen through the dawn in the early morning, every devilish phantom, sorcery, and terror (with which many a poor person, especially those wandering through the night, are often significantly frightened) vanishes. For the Devil and his household always fear the radiance of the Sun and must flee from it, so that their villainy and deceit may not be noticed or hindered by men.

## ->List of Solar Herbs.<-

### 1. Celandine / Chelidonia.

**A**lthough these three following herbs were already mentioned in the previous year, we had to cut some material short at that time and grant space to the book printer because the subject matter was becoming too extensive. The remainder, especially regarding their utility, has been saved for this year’s edition. Accordingly, there follows now what the aforementioned **Johannes Ganivetus** <note>A 15th-century French physician and astrologer, famous for his work *Amicus Medicorum*, which linked medicine with astrology.</note> further reports concerning this Celandine: namely, how one should not only process it **through a filter** <note>original: "per filtrum"; referring to a method of purification or liquid separation.</note>, but he further teaches how one can make a precious salt from it according to the **chemical art** <term>Chymischer Kunst: Early modern alchemy or spagyrics, focusing on extracting the "essential" salts and oils from organic matter.</term>. This salt is much more potent, pure, and healthy to use in all foods and dishes, especially for high potentates. He also shows how one should finally obtain the true red juice of this precious plant, and thus the most exquisite medicine from it. To translate and explain all of this here would be far too lengthy; however, whoever desires it from me may obtain a manuscript copy. Whoever drinks the distilled water and continues to do so for a time cleanses the blood thereby and is delivered from many illnesses; it especially serves against swellings of the body. This herb, placed under the bare feet and walked upon (especially in the morning), draws the evil fluids from the body downwards and is remarkably useful against **jaundice** <note>original: "Gelbsucht".</note>. The juice, when properly prepared, heals plague, cancer, and fistulas; the **sores** <note>original: "Boß"; likely referring to 'Böß' or 'Bossen', meaning malignant sores or swellings.</note> should be washed with it and the powder of the herb sprinkled therein.

# The Fourth Chapter of the Sun. ☉.

**S**OL, as if "alone" <note>original: "SOL, quasi solus"; a Latin wordplay suggesting the Sun is unique and solitary in its majesty.</note>, is the king of all planets (after God), a cause of light, day, and life; a noble officer or steward of God the Most High, from whom all other creatures must take, draw, and fetch their light, warmth, power, and beauty. Its seat in the human body is primarily in the heart, which is the first to live and the last to die in a human being, according to the opinion of some philosophers; although among the naturalists it is held that it should also command the right eye of man. It is opposed to all injustice, as well as to devilish phantoms, theft, sorcery, adultery, murder, etc., as works of darkness (Romans 13), and likewise to their accomplices: hags, witches, ghosts, etc. This is because in such clear, brightly shining light, they cannot so easily deceive poor, blinded, and ignorant people, as the work itself grandly testifies: as soon as the mighty Sun shows its rays through the dawn by the grace of the Most High, all devilish phantoms, sorcery, and terrors (by which many poor people, especially those wandering through the night, are often greatly distressed) vanish. For the devil and his household always fear the Sun's brilliance and must flee, so that their rogue's tricks and deceit are not noticed or prevented by people.

## Directory of Solar Herbs.

### 1. Celandine / *Chelidonia*.

**A**lthough these three following herbs were already mentioned in the previous year, because of the quantity of material—as it was becoming far too extensive—we had to cut it short and grant the printer some space, saving the rest, especially the practical uses, for today's volume. Therefore, what follows is what the aforementioned <term>Johannes Ganivetus</term> <note>A 15th-century French physician and astrologer known for his work "Amicus Medicorum."</note> further reports of this <term>Celandine</term> <note>Schölkraut; historically used for eye ailments and liver issues due to its yellow sap.</note>: namely, how one should not only process it through a filter <note>original: "per filtrum"</note>, but he further teaches how one can make a precious salt from it according to the <term>Chemical Art</term> <note>Chymischer Kunst; early chemistry or alchemy focused on extracting the "essential" salts and spirits of plants.</note>. This salt is much stronger, purer, and healthier to use in all food and dishes, especially for high potentates. He also shows how one might finally obtain the true juice of this precious plant, and thus the most precious medicine from it. To translate and explain all of this here would be far too extensive; however, whoever desires it from me can receive a transcript. Whoever drinks the distilled water and continues to do so for a time thereby cleanses the blood and is relieved of many illnesses; it especially serves against swellings of the body. This herb, placed under the bare feet and walked upon (especially in the morning), pulls the evil fluids down from the body and is grandly useful against <term>Jaundice</term> <note>Geelsucht; literally "yellow-sickness," associated with the Sun due to the plant's yellow sap.</note>. The juice, correctly prepared, heals <term>Plague</term>, <term>Cancer</term>, <term>Fistulas</term>, and the <term>Wolf</term> <note>Wolf; a historical term for lupus, or more generally for eating sores and severe skin chafing.</note> when washed with it and the powder is sprinkled on.

There is also the form and shape of the root—if one digs it up and examines it diligently—that it resembles a decayed, hollow tooth. Therefore, if you take it into the mouth with vinegar, or put the milky sap from the fresh leaves into a hollow tooth, you can pull it out without pain. If you rub the same sap on warts, they wither and fall off. ## 2. Mallows / Little Chalices / *Malva, Althea*.

**MALLOWS** <note>original: "Pappeln"; while this modern German word means poplars, in historical herbals it frequently referred to Mallows (*Malva*), as confirmed by the Latin synonyms provided.</note> are a particularly useful medicine for the throat and throat ulcers, or mouth rot, especially to be used in a gargle, for they soothe the unnatural heat in the throat; applied with linen cloths, they help against all hot swellings. The distilled juice of the mallows, when drunk, is a noble water for all internal hot ailments; it cools and softens all lung and side-sores <note>likely pleurisy or inflammation of the ribcage</note>, eases and heals the "red flux" <note>original: "rothe Ruhr"; an old term for dysentery</note>, hot sores of the womb in women, as well as the kidneys and bladder. This is also achieved particularly by the seed, boiled in chicken broth and drunk. Mallows laid upon hot swellings (after being crushed) along with willow leaves, or placed on wounds, truly help against inflammation.

## 3. Moly, a Herb Against All Sorcery.

**ITS** power and virtue is that it resists and drives away all sorcery if one carries it upon themselves. For this reason, King Ulysses <note>Odysseus</note> and his companions and servants could not be enchanted or harmed by the witch Circe—who used to bewitch all those who came to her—because he had received this herb from the learned artist Mercury. But what this "Moly," described by Homer, Ovid, Theophrastus, and Pliny, might actually be, is still never known. It is to be held as a poetic, fictional name, which was placed there by Homer in a poetic manner as an antidote and *Alexipharmacum* <note>a "counter-poison" or "ward against mischief"</note> to be used against all damage from sorceresses and witches. This is reflected in the name itself, for *Moly* is as much as to say a "counter-throw" or "defense," from the Greek word *Mōlyein* <note>original: Μωλύειν</note>, which means *propulsare, lenire*, that is, to drive away or to soothe. Thus, we shall let the Homeric Moly remain a poetic herb and a ward against sorcery. Dioscorides, however, in Book 3, Chapter 45, describes a specific herb which he calls Moly; it has narrow long leaves like grass, though somewhat broader, spread upon the earth. Between the leaves grows a round, hollow, smooth, high stalk; at the top of it is a knob with a thin skin, like on garlic, in which many [seeds are found...]

## Description of those animals that possess a Solar Nature.

Of this kind is now the hawk or falcon. It is not exactly a very large bird, but it has a strong, fresh heart in its body, and takes its name from robbing and taking <note>The German word for hawk, *Habicht*, was traditionally linked to *haben* (to have/take) or *happen* (to snatch)</note>, for some rob in the air, some on the earth, and they are powerfully swift and fast in flight; they can soar so high into the air in a flash that one no longer sees them, and soon they shoot down again like an arrow; they have sharp vision. Birds fear it so much that out of fear they do not know where to crawl and hide, like a deer when it is hunted by dogs. It spares no bird, not even the eagle itself. They are sometimes found together, holding each other so tightly with their claws that they fall out of the air onto the earth together.

There are many kinds of hawks, but specifically there is one species that is somewhat blackish and pale on the belly; if this one comes into a courtyard, it eats up all the pigeons and chickens. These one must diligently pursue, treat them as rogues, and kill them how and with whatever one can. It is not easy to shoot, for it trusts no long stick that has a round hole in the front; yet it does not fear the little tube, but rather the little ball that crawls out. All this is caused by its bad conscience, for a thief and a murderer must always live in fear, and they can never leave their thievery and murder even if the beadle <note>a minor court official</note> or the executioner with the sword stood behind them. It knows that stealing is forbidden upon pain of hanging, and murder upon pain of the sword. 

Once a young nobleman in the country wanted to take revenge on one that had flown into the house after a chicken; he received this unexpected, impudent guest thus: he closed all doors and windows and bid him a "friendly welcome," chased him around the house, seized him, hacked off the feathers in his wings, and set him in the room. He thought the hawk would behave himself piously and properly, as is fitting for a prisoner; but that was what they call **committing the sheep to the wolf** <note>original Latin: "ovem lupo committere"</note> and setting the goat to be the gardener. Now the nobleman had many young chicks in the room, all of which the hawk ate, and the old mother hen as well. Thus he [the hawk] wasted the mercy shown to him entirely; he had to pay for the meal and was beaten stone dead, for nature does not depart from its nature <note>original: "Art läst von Art nicht"; a proverb meaning inherent character cannot be changed</note>. Otherwise, however, they are trained for the hunt, especially for fowling or for partridges, which they like to eat and which people also like to eat. But how should one catch them? 1. With a trap, in which one places a white pigeon or chicken and pours out food for them. When a hungry brother comes flying out of the forest and notices or catches sight of this chicken or pigeon, he shoots down upon it and thus catches himself.
