## The remaining part of a Solar History follows, of which mention was made last year.

**O**nce, they appointed seven or eight men by night who were intended to kill the aforementioned Lysis; but he, along with another, defended himself so bravely that four of them remained dead on the spot. Since his rivals then saw that nothing could be achieved against him by force, they denounced him to the aforementioned King of France regarding his illicit affair <note>original: "Bulschafft"; implying a romantic or sexual relationship outside of marriage</note> with Sylvia. When Lysander later came before the King, the King looked upon him with displeasure, scolded him, and reproached him most severely for allowing another man to go to his wife. He commanded him to depart from his presence and not to appear before him again until he had avenged such a shame. The King’s words caused Lysander great pain, and he knew not how to answer; instead, he traveled home, took a strong poison, mixed it with water, and went with it early to Sylvia’s bed. He woke her, presented her with the cause of his grievance, and commanded her to choose one of these three things: namely, either to drink the poison, or to be stabbed with the dagger he held in his hands, or to write to Lysis that Lysander was not at home and that—if he loved her at all—he should come to her immediately. Sylvia attempted to excuse herself and Lysis, and was even willing to die rather than deliver an innocent man to the butcher's block <note>original: "Fleischbanck"; a metaphor for certain death or slaughter</note>. But when she saw that Master Lysander was pointing the weapon at her breast, she loved her own life more than that of Lysis; she therefore begged for mercy, took paper and pen, and wrote the following letter to Lysis, which Lysander dictated to her:

> If you love me, most beloved Lysis, as you have always given me proof, then you will not fail to come tomorrow to comfort a sorrowful soul who is dying of longing to see you. The absence of Lysander should encourage you in this, as he will not return for several days. I await you with as much impatience as you are worthy of. Good day, my dear treasure; do not delay in pleasing us both.

When it was finished, Lysander gave the letter to a lackey <note>original: "Laggen"; an archaic term for a servant or footman</note> who had been trained for this purpose. He delivered it to Lysis, who was quite joyful at this news, because he believed he would now finally obtain that which all lovers seek with such great passion; he therefore immediately set out alone with the lackey. However, when he reached the gate of the castle, a shudder <note>original: "Grausen"</note> came over him. He said to the lackey that he did not know what was wrong with him; something was telling him that he should come to his lady at another time, for he was dreaming, as it were, of a betrayal. The lackey helped him to stay in good spirits and to fear no evil. He therefore rode on. When he entered the castle courtyard, he was received by those who usually received him. One took his horse, the second his cloak, the third his sword <note>original: "Degen"</note>, and so he went unarmed up to his lover’s chamber. As soon as she saw him, she shrieked aloud and fell upon her bed in a faint.

### The Fifth Chapter: Concerning Venus. ♀.

VENUS is the 5th planet to be counted in order from Saturn downwards; it received its name among the Latins *from loveliness* <note>original Latin: "à venustate"; derived from "venustas," meaning charm, beauty, or grace.</note>, which is to say, from beauty or brilliance, because it is such an exceedingly beautiful, brightly shining planet, being almost as beautiful as Jupiter, though a little smaller. However, the ancient heathens [represented] through such a beautiful woman debauchery <note>original: "Bulerey"; referring to illicit romantic affairs or sexual license.</note>, through her blind...

But the child **Cupid** <note>original: "Cupidinem"</note>, representing childish blind desire or the suitor, lover, or mistress, etc., and the two turtledoves representing mating, spouses, or companions, were intended by the ancients to depict amorous persons. With the beautiful, bright radiance surrounding the chariot, they wished to indicate that all suitors and lovers desire to be seen and held as rich, stately, beautiful, and magnificent, even if deceit and beggary lie hidden behind the facade. The colors associated with her [Venus's] plants are beautiful, lovely, joyful, merry, lustful <note>In this context, the original German "geil" refers to lush growth, fertility, and vigor.</note>, bold, charming, lily-white, and green. ## A Catalog of Venusian Herbs

### 1. Frankincense / *Thus* <note>original: "Thus," the Latin term for frankincense.</note>

**Frankincense** is an Arabian, white, round, fatty gum flowing from the tree of **Lebanon** <term>original: *Libano*; the frankincense tree, *Boswellia sacra*</term>. For this reason, it is also called **Olibanum** <term>original: *Olibanum*; a pharmaceutical name for high-quality frankincense</term> in pharmacies. When placed upon glowing coals, it smokes quite well. It is good when used on wounds, for it draws them together so effectively that one does not need to stitch them, and it prevents putrid flesh from growing therein. **Olibanum** drunk with wine is good for those who spit blood and for all fluxes of blood in both men and women. Mixed with vinegar and oil and applied with milk to ulcers of the anus, it heals them. Dropped into the ears with sweet wine, it removes the ringing therein. The smoke of **Olibanum** is good for aching eyes when allowed to drift into them. It takes away sadness, increases reason, strengthens the heart, and creates joyful blood. The bark of the Frankincense tree yields a smoke like the resin itself, but stronger. It is good when drunk for spitting blood and for the flowing of the reproductive organs when used as a suppository. The soot of Frankincense is made by burning it in an earthen pot with holes. It is also good for heat in the eyes, fluxes, putrid ulcers, and deep cancers.

The soot of such virtuous substances is also made from **Myrtle** and **Styrax** <note>A fragrant resin obtained from trees in the *Styrax* genus, often used in perfumes and medicine.</note>. Black Frankincense is called **Thymiama** <term>original: *Thymiama*; a Greek-derived term for incense or a fumigating powder</term> in the pharmacies; it is used in incense powders to guard against poisonous, foul air.

### 2. Noble Liverwort / *Trifolium Aureum* <note>original: *Trifolium Aureum*, literally "Golden Trefoil."</note>

**It is to be known** that everything which, through its flower, herb, or root, etc., **prefigures** <term>original: *præfigurirt*; this refers to the "Doctrine of Signatures," a historical belief that plants resembling body parts can heal those parts</term> and reflects a limb or organ in the human body, has—by a unique influence—a strong drive or **motion** <note>original: "motum"</note> into that prefigured member, to strengthen its **essence** <note>original: "essentz"</note> and its power. Thus, the **Noble Liverwort**, as well as the **Stone Liverwort**, prefigures the liver in humans and cattle. Therefore, the conclusion to be drawn is that such herbs must be exceedingly beneficial to the liver.

This Noble Liverwort produces leaves like **Asarabacca** <note>original: "Haselwurz," a low-growing plant with kidney-shaped leaves.</note>, each divided into three parts. It has small, hairy stems and produces white or light blue violets in March, similar to **Wood Sorrel** <note>original: "Buchampffer."</note>, each also on its own stem. From these come rough little pods which bear seeds elongated like those of the **Columbine** <note>original: "Agleyen."</note>.

This herb, boiled in wine, opens a congested liver and promotes the flow of urine, cleanses the kidneys and bladder, is healing for all types of injuries, and quenches all sorts of internal heat. The water distilled from it [the hydrosol] performs the same function.

### 3. Rock Liverwort / *Hepatica* <note>original: *Hepatica*</note>

**R**ock Liverwort—also called Spring Liverwort, Rock Lichen, or Moss Lichen—is called *Leichēn* <note>original: Λειχὴν</note> in Greek and *Lichen* <note>original: *Lichen*</note> in Latin. Commonly, it is named *Hepatica*.
It grows in cold, damp, shaded places and upon stones near springs. It is a creeping, green, succulent, coarse herb without a stalk, flower, or seed; however, several stalks grow out from between the leaves, topped with small star-shaped heads. These are not true stalks or flowers, but rather fibers or small hairs.

This green, creeping little liverwort, boiled in wine or otherwise drunk, heals an injured lung and liver, resists all heat, and stills the "head-flow" <note>likely catarrh or rheum, an excess of mucus flowing from the head to the throat</note> that constantly falls into the throat. It is more useful than it is generally esteemed for many bodily ailments arising from heat.

## Description of the Venusian Animals, among which the Turtledove particularly belongs.

**T**urtur <note>original: *Turtur*</note>, a turtledove, takes its name from its voice or its cry—from the sound of the voice <note>original: *à vocis sono*</note>. These are gray, dark, small doves, and the very smallest among all pigeons. They lay only two eggs, occasionally three, but they only hatch two. When they drink, they act like other doves; they do not draw back and hold their neck upward to let the water run down the throat, but rather they quickly drink their fill into the throat <note>Unlike most birds that must tilt their heads back to swallow, pigeons and doves can suction water continuously, a detail the author finds noteworthy</note>. It is a lovely creature, and for this reason, a head of a household likes to have them in the home for the sake of dear marriage, of which they are the picture and image. When taken from the nest and raised, they become quite tame and domesticated. I once had one that was so tame that I placed it with me on a wagon and later locked it in a chest. It never desired to fly away from me.

In the fields, it keeps to the medium-sized thistles that have brown flowers on top and are not very tall; it is near these that they are caught with nets around harvest time. In an aviary, or when kept otherwise inside the home, they do not produce young like other pigeons, nor do quails or partridges.

...whom I shall leave unnamed and call *Alidor*, had conducted himself quite often and very well in the *French* Wars: so much so, that by the 22nd year of his age, he had already become famous and led a *company* of cavalry <term>Compagnie Reuter; a standard unit of horsemen in the 16th and 17th centuries</term> against King Henry the Fourth, who was at that time still the King of *Navarre*. It happened, however, that another nobleman, his neighbor and a Colonel over a regiment of *Spanish* people named *Lycidas*, also returned home from the Netherlands and was visited and received by the nobility in great numbers. *Alidor* did not wish to be the last among them, but likewise visited the aforementioned *Lycidas* at his home, accompanied by a nobleman, his attendant, named *Fatyme*. The goddess *Venus* used the lady's eyes to set all noble hearts aflame, and her brow was a true painting in which all the *Graces* <note>original: *Gratiæ*; in Greek and Roman mythology, these are the goddesses of charm, beauty, and creativity</note> were *represented*. As soon as the two of them merely looked at one another, they were immediately inflamed with a great love for each other. *Lycidas*, who suspected no evil of his consort, commanded her to entertain *Alidor* while he went to meet another *company* that had arrived to greet him. When these two were thus left alone, the love in both their hearts first began to grow; nevertheless, *Alidor* did not dare to reveal it until the beautiful *Calliree*, who saw his *passion* in his face, gave him cause and opportunity through her speech. 

While they were in such lovely conversation and each revealed their love to the other, *Lycidas* stepped into the room with the aforementioned *company*; *Calliree* went to meet them, received them, and prepared a *collation* <term>A light meal or refreshment served to guests</term> for them. When this was finished, the others returned home; *Alidor*, however, had in the meantime sent his nobleman *Fatyme* home and commanded him not to return until toward evening. Now that all the others were gone, *Alidor* acted very annoyed that *Fatyme* had stayed away so long and behaved as if he intended to ride home alone. 

But *Lycidas* would not let him depart, but rather begged him to stay with him overnight. And so that the time might pass more quickly for *Alidor*, he and *Calliree* led him into the garden; and thus he himself again gave these two lovers the opportunity to speak with one another. And as he had other business to attend to, *Calliree* drew a *diamond* from her finger and presented it to *Alidor*, while he gave her a *ruby*: and so these two swore eternal loyalty to one another. They also devised the means by which they might constantly write and send *post* <note>original: *Post*; here referring to a private messenger or secret correspondence system</note> to one another: namely, that *Fatyme* should pay court to a noble lady-in-waiting named *Iris*, whom *Calliree* could well trust, by which means he might constantly come to *Calliree* and bring her the secret *messages* from *Alidor*. There you would see all the suffering that love can cause a mortal man to feel, who hopes for no other salvation from the pain caused by a wretched absence than from a most beloved presence: meanwhile, however, because I await such blessedness, I pray you to testify to me through your writing the memory you have of him, whose fortune and welfare hang alone upon your beautiful eyes.

He gave this letter to Fatyme, with the request that he should secretly deliver it to his mistress under the pretense of wanting to visit his <term>Bulschafft; a sweetheart or love-interest</term> Iris. Fatyme traveled with the letter and met Lycidas right at the castle just as he was about to ride out early for the hunt. He performed his bow and greeting. Lycidas said he knew his intent well, and that he should just boldly go to his sweetheart, whom he would find in the castle. He did so, and Iris, who had been informed of his arrival, came to meet him and received him; afterwards, however, she also announced his arrival to her lady: who immediately sprang out of bed, let Fatyme come to her, received the letter from him, and before she read it, presented him with 100 <term>Pistolen; gold coins, also known as Spanish Doubloons</term> as a messenger's reward. When she had read the letter, she had a breakfast prepared for Fatyme, during which time she finished the reply with which Fatyme intended to travel back. But because Lycidas returned from the hunt during that time, he kept him for the midday meal and entertained him the whole time with his sweetheart. When that was finished, Fatyme once again played so lovelily upon the lute that Lycidas asked him to visit him very often: which he then did to the particular pleasure of both the aforementioned persons. This time, however, he traveled away towards evening and delivered to Alidor—who had waited for him with great longing—Calliree’s letter, the content of which was this:

> My dear treasure, if it stood as well in my power to release you from the martyrdom of which you complain as I have the will to do so, believe that you should soon be delivered.

I pray you / that you would consider / that even the slightest suspicion / which should arise in the heart of my husband / who is suspicious by nature / would be enough / to ruin us. Console yourself with the hope / that I have / that we shall soon see each other again with pleasure / and indeed with more opportunity / than we have had until now. In the meantime, send your Fatyme to me often / so that / when opportunity arises / I can remind you of it. Commended to GOD, my most beloved treasure: Keep always the memory of her / who alone has life from the belief / that You love her. Thus Fatyme played his part exceedingly well: But because he came almost daily / and Encidas could not believe / that it happened for Iris's sake alone; especially because Calliree began to become quite sad: Encidas took good care to watch his wife / and finally found a letter / that Alidor had written to her / which he placed back in its spot / so that Calliree could not notice it. Though for this reason he would have gladly punished his wife / and Alidor too / with their lives: But because he had previously loved Calliree inwardly: and also knew well / that Alidor, on account of his bravery and noble lineage / was held in high regard / he therefore looked rather upon love and danger / and returned with Calliree to the Netherlands / so that he might take away the opportunity / for these two to love one another: which then seemed strange to Calliree: But Alidor fell into such distress over it / that he often wished to take his own life: which Fatyme, however, always prevented.

### Chapter 6 concerning Mercury. ☿.

<term>Mercury</term> is a small and bright star / not too shining / and white / which stands closest to the sun: The colors / which are attributed to him / are mixed and manifold. He is of an unstable nature and quality / for with good planets he is good / and with the evil ones, evil / with the masculine he is masculine / with the feminine he is feminine / with the fortunate he is fortunate / with the unfortunate, unlucky / with the moist, moist / with the warm he is warm / and is, so to speak, like a <term>Zwierer; a dual-natured being or someone who adapts to others</term> / yet his own nature is moist and dry.

### Registry of Mercurial Herbs.
### 1. Marjoram.

Since mention was already made of this and the following herbs in the previous year / yet at that time / due to the quantity of material / because it tended to be far too extensive / it had to be somewhat shortened / and some space granted to the printer / the rest / especially the usefulness / was saved until this year's edition. Thus follows now concerning <term>Marjoram water</term>. The leaves stripped from the stem and distilled <note>original: "gebrandt" — referring to the process of creating a medicinal "water" through distillation</note> / at the end of May / Drunk / in the morning and evening at three <term>Loth; a historical unit of weight, approximately 15-17 grams</term> / and the head rubbed with it / is good for a cold head / drives out the urine and the tearing stone <note>referring to the sharp pain of kidney or bladder stones</note> / strengthens and warms the cold womb—

<summary>This page concludes a romantic narrative of secret correspondence and discovery before transitioning into an astrological and botanical treatise on the properties of Mercury and the medicinal uses of marjoram water.</summary>

...her / brings to women their time <note>Refers to the promotion of menstruation.</note> / drives away the whites <note>A historical term for leukorrhea or vaginal discharge.</note> and strengthens the womb. It is good for stroke / restores lost speech / and if held often in the mouth / it brings back the memory / and strengthens the brain and head. Drunk / and rubbed upon the body / it strengthens the heart / disperses the evil humors around the chest / drives away the evil flows of the head / and cures the sniffles.

## 2. Juniper.

Now follows further / what use the noble <term>juniper</term> shrub brings and causes in medicine. Juniper berries eaten on an empty stomach / give a good scent to the breath / preserve a person from bad air <note>Historically believed to be "miasma" or foul air that carried disease.</note> / also cause good urination / and open and loosen the bowels. Also / whoever eats five or six juniper berries on an empty stomach every morning for an entire year / shall drive away the stone <note>Kidney or bladder stones.</note> in the body / if he had it / but if it had not yet grown in him / then it shall never grow in him; yet no one should neglect / to eat juniper berries in the morning / for their virtues cannot all be described. Juniper boiled in wine / and drunk / is good for the stomach / also against all ailments of the chest / for coughs and abdominal cramps / and for whoever has poison in them / says <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and author of a foundational herbal text.</note> / it shall help them; the juice of the berries / drunk for snakebite / and placed upon the wound mixed with wine / helps / and the juice of the leaves does the same.

## 3. Lungwort / Pulmonaria.

<term>Lungwort</term> <note>original: "Pulmonaria." This plant was named for its spotted leaves, which early physicians believed resembled diseased lungs.</note> is a growth found on oak or beech trees / also on stone cliffs / with soft leaves / lying tightly upon one another / and full of holes / yellow underneath / resembling a lung. It is by nature moist and cold in the second degree <note>A classification from Galenic medicine, which balanced the body's "humors" using plants with opposite qualities.</note>. Its virtue is to soften and dissolve. Lungwort / which grows on beech trees / is said to be better than the other kind. The cold, moist, creeping <term>liverwort</term> <note>original: "Leberkraut." Likely referring to Marchantiophyta, which grows on damp rocks.</note> / found on wet rocks, as well as the cold lungwort on oak trees / may both be used together internally and externally. The other creeping forest-herb on oak trees / is also commonly used for <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "Lungensucht," a general term for wasting lung diseases like tuberculosis.</note> / for wheezing / shortness of breath / and for coughing / made into a powder / or the distilled water drunk. Make the powder of this herb thus: Take dried lungwort / aniseed <note>original: "Entsamen."</note> / fox lungs dried in the air <note>In "sympathetic medicine," it was believed that using the lungs of a long-winded animal like a fox could cure human breathing ailments.</note> / fennel seed / licorice / elecampane root / ginger / an equal amount of each / and each pounded well separately / and sifted / then add as much of the best sugar / as all the other ingredients weigh together / mixed together and used like other medicinal powders <note>original: "Tresenen."</note> evening and morning / it drives away wheezing / opens the lungs and liver / and is good for a rough cough.

## List of Animals / that have a Mercurial Nature.

The <term>guenon</term> <note>original: "Meerkatze," literally "sea-cat," a term for various long-tailed monkeys.</note> is another kind of ape / for they also have hairy, round heads / and do not look very different from humans. Their neck is as thick as the head / therefore one does not bind them by the neck / but around the loins or flanks. They have a long, strong tail / and are very clever / wanting to imitate everything humans do. When the moon is new they are sad / but when it is full / they rejoice.

# Mercurial History of a French Captain
## named Guillery.

**T**his Guillery was born in Lower Brittany in France and came from a noble family. Because he was a diligent student in his youth, he was sent by his parents to Rennes <note>original: "Renes"</note> to <term>continue</term> <note>From the Latin *continuare*, meaning to persist in his education.</note> his studies there. When he reached the 18th year of his age, he was greatly feared by all the students there; no homicide or brawl occurred at night without him being accused of it, as he was always found at the forefront of every wicked company. When his father heard of this, he intended to restrain him from such an evil life, but Guillery cared nothing for his father’s or anyone else’s warnings. Instead, he grew worse the longer he stayed. 

Because war broke out in France between King Henry IV and the Duke of Savoy <note>This refers to the Franco-Savoyard War of 1600–1601.</note>, Guillery joined the conflict and displayed his heroic courage in many ways. Consequently, because of his excellent deeds, he received a commission and was given command of a <term>Company</term> <note>original: "Compagnia"; a military unit of infantry.</note>. However, shortly thereafter, this war was settled and peace was made, and everyone was ordered to return home. Guillery, who did not wish to return to his former life or move back home, considered what he might do in the future. Therefore, he summoned about 40 daring fellows to him, to whom he explained the terms of the peace and how he had resolved to support himself in the future. To these men, such a <term>proposal</term> <note>original: "propositum"; a plan or resolution.</note> was not disagreeable. They pledged their assistance to him with an oath and began their business by robbing those whom they encountered. 

At first, Guillery and his aforementioned followers stayed in Saintonge <note>original: "Sanctonia, Xaintoigne or Sainctonge"; a historical province on the western coast of France.</note> and the surrounding lands, and because of this trade of his, he soon became known throughout all of France. Many who had known him in the recent war marveled that such a brave Captain had become such a great robber. When news of this was brought to his father, he died of grief within a few days, leaving behind another son of 19 years old, who, after his father’s death, also went to join his brother. 

Thus, Guillery carried on his business vigorously. Among other events, he once met a peasant on the road going from Niort to La Rochelle <note>Two major towns in western France; La Rochelle was a significant port and stronghold.</note> who was traveling to Rochelle regarding a legal matter. Guillery joined this man, and when he understood from the peasant that he had no money, he said that they were both in the same state, as he also had no money. However, he told the peasant that he should kneel down with him and call upon God for money. The peasant had to do this three or four times, and each time, Guillery pulled something out of his own pocket, pretending he had received it from God, and shared it with the peasant. 

But while the peasant also searched in his own pockets, he could never find anything. Finally, Guillery himself searched him everywhere and found 150 gold crowns, which the peasant had intended to carry to Rochelle for his aforementioned legal case. Guillery divided these into two parts, gave half to the peasant, and said to him: "Take the

<summary>This page recounts the early life and military career of Guillery, a French nobleman who turned to banditry after the Franco-Savoyard War, and includes an anecdote about him tricking a peasant.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Mercurial History" of the French brigand Guillery, illustrating the "mercurial" traits of cunning and theft. It then transitions to the final chapter on the Seven Planets, focusing on the Moon (Luna) and its associated botanical properties.</meta>

half the portion that God has bestowed upon you. I see that God loves you, because he has sent you so much money at once. And with that, he took his leave from the farmer and let him go his way, who was also happy to have escaped so well. Now, Guillery performed many such little tricks; he also craftily lured distinguished people—among them the Chief Justice of Rochelle and the Governor of Niort—into the Forest of la Chasteniere, where he and his men regularly stayed. Although he would release them, he kept their <term>cassocks</term> <note>original: "Casaquen," a type of long, heavy coat often worn by soldiers or officials.</note>, which he then used as a disguise to capture a castle where he obtained great wealth. However, since the two aforementioned gentlemen from Rochelle and Niort did not want to let this <term>affront</term> <note>original: "Affront oder Bravade"</note> go unavenged, they eventually spied on Guillery and surrounded him with their men. Although he defended himself bravely and eventually escaped, his aforementioned younger brother was captured and afterwards <term>broken on the wheel</term> <note>original: "geradbrecht"; a brutal form of execution used for high-profile criminals.</note> while still alive at Saintes, along with two or three other companions. 

This news greatly troubled Guillery; he wept bitterly for the death of his brother. Because his conscience was awakened, and he considered that he had already been captured once before and had only escaped through cunning, he feared he might be captured again and receive the same punishment as his brother. Therefore, he sought to lead a better life in an unknown place from then on. He summoned his remaining companions—about 15 or 16 of them—to a convenient location, reminded them of the life they had led and what had happened to the others and his own brother, and exhorted them to change their lives in the future. Finding them willing, he gave each a sum of money and sent them off in different directions. He kept only two of his most trusted men with him, with whom he made his way toward Bourdeaux dressed in noble attire. From there, he went to Saint Justin, intending to live there as a remote place. Because of his qualities, politeness, and generosity, he soon became acquainted with the nobility; he even married a young widow, with whom he lived for three or four years. Next year <note>A common phrase in serial publications of the time, suggesting more of the story will follow in a later volume.</note>, more on the unfortunate end of this history.

### The seventh and last Chapter: Of the Moon. ☽

**L**una, or the Moon, is the very last, lowest, and closest planet to us inhabitants of Earth. Among the poets, she has many names: in the waters she is called **Diana**, in the heavens **Luna**, and in the underworld **Proserpina**. Also **Lucina**, as the one who gives light to those being born, preserves all things, and brings them forth. In the human body, her primary seat is in the **bladder**, which becomes completely round [like the full moon]; otherwise, she governs all water—indeed, the entire mighty sea. She also rules the left eye in men and the right eye in women, as well as the belly, intestines, and the left side. The colors of the things she grows are varied: white, green, ash-gray, sometimes bluish and yellowish; they generally have thick, soft, and juicy leaves.

### Registry of Lunar Herbs / 1. Linden / Tilia.

**L**inden <note>original: "Linden," the Lime tree.</note> takes its name from its gentleness <note>original: "Lindigkeit"; the author is making a wordplay on "Lind" (gentle/soft) and "Linde" (Linden).</note>; in Latin it is called **Tilia**. Its leaves resemble ivy, yet are softer, more pointed in their roundness, and finely notched. The blossom is green at first, but when it comes out, it is yellow. The fruit is like small ivy berries; when they shed in the month of August, round, black, and sweet seeds fall out. The trunk is thick, the bark thick and black, under which is a white, thin, and tough skin <note>This refers to the "bast" or inner bark, used historically for making cords.</note> full of sap, tough and sweet. Its wood is gentle and soft. It is a very fruitful tree, for it spreads its branches more than other trees, so that it is often supported by many columns. There was such a tree at Mainz <note>original: "Mäing"</note> in the Monastery of **St. Alban**, supported by large stone columns, yet it was still pruned annually.

## 2. Garden Poppy / *Papaver*.

**P**oppy <note>original: "Magsamen," literally "stomach-seed," a common early modern name for the poppy</note> is called *Papaver* and *Papaver sativum* in Latin, otherwise known as oil-poppy, oil-seed, or *Mohn*. There are two primary types of this poppy, namely the white and the black, distinguished by their flowers and seeds. The white poppy has white flowers; the black has blackish-red flowers, or sometimes ash-colored and white mixed together. It has rough, broad, jagged, ash-colored leaves, similar to curly lettuce. They hold their seeds in round heads—well known to everyone—and are planted in fields and gardens. Any person may take note of other distinctions among poppies based on their flowers and seeds. The poppy heads are called *Kōdeia* <note>original: "Κιο-δεια"</note> by the Greeks, which is why an *electuary* <note>a medicinal paste made with honey or syrup</note> made from poppy heads is called *Diacodium*. From the poppy heads, one collects a juice by scoring them with a knife; this is called *Opium*, which is used in several preparations <note>original: "Compositionibus"</note> of medicines called *Opiates & Confections*, which have their own specific use in medicine. A juice is also squeezed out of crushed poppy heads, which is called *Meconium*, though it is not as strong as *Opium*. The white poppy is cold and moist in nature, but the black is cold and dry. The seeds are collected in summer when they are ripe; they remain preserved for five years. From both types of seeds, one makes a "sleep-plaster," mixed with breast milk and egg whites; when applied to the temples <note>original: "auff den Schlaff," referring to the anatomical temple</note>, it induces good sleep.

## 3. Leek / *Porrum*.

**A**ll leek herbs are warm in the third degree <note>a reference to the Galenic system of humors, where the "third degree" indicates a very strong quality</note>. When crushed with salt and applied to "evil blisters" <note>likely pustules or sores</note>, they heal them. Eaten raw, leeks are like poison and bloat the belly. If used too much, they cause headaches, bring bad dreams and thirst, weigh down the stomach, inflame the blood, and cause one to waste away. Eaten leeks promote a woman's period and stimulate urination. Leek juice taken with breast milk is good for coughs and lung ailments. Taken in a drink, it is for spitting blood and nosebleeds. Eaten raw, it causes a loss of appetite but dispels drunkenness and makes for a clear voice. Applied as a plaster to bruised limbs, it settles swelling and dissolves clotted blood. Drinking the juice relieves sciatica <note>original: "Hüfftwehe"</note>, old coughs, chest and lung pain, and dropsy <note>original: "Wassersucht," an old term for swelling caused by fluid retention</note>. It is for swellings of a woman’s "secret parts" <note>genitalia</note>; it heals unclean, cold wounds, and when poured therein, it consumes "foul flesh" <note>necrotic or proud flesh</note>. For poisonous bites, the juice is drunk with honey and the leaves are laid over the wound.
The shallot <note>original: "Eschlauch"</note> is also of a cold and poisonous nature, good for neither the healthy nor the sick to eat. However, whoever wishes to eat it should first soak it in wine for two or three days. Shallots should be used by those burdened with fevers <note>original: "Febres"</note>. He who has a bad stomach and eats shallots will find his pain increased. All leeks, when eaten, do no harm, except for the shallot. Sick people burdened with too much moisture <note>phlegm</note> should avoid leeks.

### A List of those Animals subject to the Moon, among which the Crayfish especially belongs.

**T**he Latins call crayfish *Cancros*, or *Cancros fluviates*. There are now many kinds of them, especially in the sea, to the extent that it is written that in the Indian Sea <note>referring to the Indian Ocean</note> such crayfish are to be found that are four cubits <note>roughly six feet</note> long. But among us, there are simple crayfish in lakes, ponds, and flowing waters, which hide under the roots of alder stumps standing by the shore and in holes at the edge. There, one must sometimes pull them out with the hands, regardless of the fact that one sometimes brings out a human hand <note>a dark joke or warning about finding drowned bodies while reaching into underwater holes</note> instead of a crayfish. If one throws a horse’s head or an ox’s head from the knacker’s yard <note>original: "Schindanger," where animal carcasses were processed</note> into water where crayfish are present, they will crawl all over it. He must have been a bold man who ate the first crayfish, although it is not an unhealthy food; they are very healthy for consumptive people <note>those suffering from wasting diseases like tuberculosis</note>, though they are difficult to digest and harmful to the eyes. However, they provide good nourishment to the body. When their shells are burnt to ashes and drunk with honey and Gentian, it heals those who have been bitten by a mad dog <note>rabies</note>.

...of the forehead, they facilitate sleep.

## 3. Leek / *Porrum*.

**L**eek is called *Porrum* in Latin and *Lauch* in German. It has a round, bulbous root, and the stems are thick, white, and full of juice. At the top, it produces a round head containing black seeds. There are two primary kinds: one is the head-leek <note>original: "Heuptlauch," likely the common leek</note> or *Capitatum*; the other is the cut-leek or *Sectile* <note>a variety of leek or chive meant to be harvested by cutting the leaves</note>. The leek is hot and dry in the third degree <term>third degree: a classification in Galenic medicine indicating a very strong effect on the body's humors</term>. It produces bad, thick, and scorched blood, as well as <insert>black</insert> bile or melancholy <term>melancholy: one of the four humors, associated here with "black bile" which was thought to cause sadness or irritability</term>. It causes flatulence and sends many vapors to the head, which results in troubled and terrifying dreams.

## 4. Wild Radish or Wild Cress / *Raphanus sylvestris*.

**T**his plant is commonly called *Meerrettich* <note>original: "Meerrettich," modern German for horseradish</note> and is well known to everyone. It is hot and dry in the third degree. It thins and expels phlegmatic humors <note>original: "Phlegmatischen Feuchtigkeiten," referring to the humor associated with mucus and coldness</note>, stimulates the stomach, and opens obstructions of the internal organs.

## 5. Crayfish / *Astacus*.

**T**he crayfish is called *Astacus* or *Astakos* in Greek, *Cancer* in Latin, and *Krebs* in German. Regarding its relationship to the Moon, it is certain that all shellfish and those with hard shells increase or decrease in flesh as the Moon waxes and wanes. The crayfish is cold and moist in nature. Its ashes are used against the bite of a mad dog <note>a historical reference to rabies</note> and for the "wasting sickness" <note>original: "Dörrsucht," referring to consumption or tuberculosis</note> of the lungs. The Moon has its "house" or primary residence in the sign of the <term>Cancer: the fourth sign of the zodiac, represented by the crab</term>.

Paper marbling is a method of aqueous surface design which produces patterns similar to smooth marble or other kinds of stone. In historical bookbinding, these decorative papers were used both for their beauty and to protect the internal pages from wear and handling.</note>

<summary>This page is a blank flyleaf featuring a decorative marbled border, serving as a physical and aesthetic transition within the manuscript's structure.</summary>
<keywords>marbled paper, blank page, manuscript binding, decorative border, endpaper</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of blank endpapers. It serves as a structural buffer between the binding/marbled edges and the beginning of the text proper.</meta>

[This page is a blank leaf and contains no written text.]

<note>In historical manuscript production, several blank pages (known as flyleaves) were commonly included at the start and end of a volume. These pages acted as a protective barrier, shielding the primary text from the wear and tear of the heavy covers. They also offered a convenient space for later owners to record their names, shelfmarks for a library, or brief indices of the contents.</note>

<summary>This page is a blank flyleaf, continuing the structural sequence of the manuscript's binding and providing a protective buffer for the content to come.</summary>

<keywords>blank page, flyleaf, bookbinding, manuscript structure, preservation</keywords>

### Decorative Endpaper

->[Peacock Marbled Pattern]<-

<note>This page is a fine example of decorative marbling, specifically a "peacock" or "comb" pattern. To create this effect, a craftsman would float grey, mustard yellow, and reddish-brown pigments on a liquid surface (often a thickened water called "size") and use a comb-like tool to drag the colors into fan-like swirls. The paper was then carefully laid on top to lift the design. In traditional bookbinding, these patterns were used for endpapers—the sheets that join the book's pages to its cover—both for aesthetic appeal and to mask the glue and structural materials of the binding.</note>

<summary>This page consists of a decorative marbled pattern in a peacock or comb style, used as a traditional endpaper in historical bookbinding.</summary>

<keywords>marbled paper, peacock pattern, comb marbling, bookbinding, endpaper, decorative arts</keywords>
